<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 02-48 (biopsy and neurologic tests.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, histological features, and genetic basis of **Congenital Myasthenic Syndromes (CMS)**, specifically differentiating it from other neuromuscular disorders based on clinical presentation, electrodiagnostic findings, and muscle biopsy characteristics. (考察先天性肌无力综合征 (CMS) 的病理生理学、组织学特征和遗传基础，并将其与其他神经肌肉疾病区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with progressive ptosis and generalized muscle weakness, particularly affecting proximal limb muscles and bulbar function (difficulty swallowing, slurred speech). His symptoms began shortly after birth and have worsened gradually. He has no history of fever, rash, or recent infections. Family history is significant for similar symptoms in his mother, who was diagnosed with "muscle weakness" in childhood but never had specific testing. Neurological examination reveals bilateral ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, facial weakness, and proximal muscle weakness (4/5 strength). Deep tendon reflexes are normal. Electromyography (EMG) shows normal motor nerve conduction velocities and normal compound muscle action potentials (CMAPs) at rest. However, repetitive nerve stimulation (RNS) at 3 Hz reveals a decremental response of >10% in the quadriceps muscle, which is partially improved by post-exercise facilitation. A muscle biopsy from the quadriceps shows normal muscle fiber size and morphology, with no evidence of inflammation, necrosis, or fiber type grouping. However, special stains reveal prominent accumulation of acetylcholine receptors (AChRs) in the synaptic cleft region, appearing as dense clusters. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *RAPSN* gene.

What is the most likely underlying molecular defect causing this patient's condition?

(A) Deficiency in the enzyme acetylcholinesterase, leading to prolonged ACh presence in the synaptic cleft.
(B) Autoimmune destruction of postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction.
(C) Defective synthesis or clustering of acetylcholine receptors at the postsynaptic membrane.
(D) Impaired release of acetylcholine from the presynaptic nerve terminal.
(E) Mutation in the gene encoding the voltage-gated sodium channel, leading to impaired nerve conduction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age of onset and progression:** 3-year-old boy with symptoms since birth, progressive weakness. Suggests a congenital or early-onset disorder.
*   **Symptoms:** Ptosis, generalized weakness (proximal, bulbar). Classic symptoms of neuromuscular junction (NMJ) or muscle disorders.
*   **Family history:** Mother with similar symptoms. Suggests a genetic basis.
*   **Neurological exam:** Ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, facial weakness, proximal weakness, normal reflexes. Consistent with NMJ or muscle disease, but normal reflexes argue against primary motor neuron or peripheral nerve disease.
*   **EMG/RNS:** Normal nerve conduction, normal CMAPs at rest, decremental response on RNS (suggests NMJ defect), partial improvement with post-exercise facilitation (characteristic of some CMS subtypes).
*   **Muscle biopsy:** Normal fiber size/morphology, no inflammation/necrosis. Rules out many myopathies and inflammatory conditions. Prominent AChR accumulation in synaptic cleft region. This is a key finding pointing towards a defect in AChR function or clustering.
*   **Genetic testing:** *RAPSN* gene mutation. *RAPSN* encodes rapsyn, a protein crucial for AChR clustering at the postsynaptic membrane.

These clues collectively point towards a Congenital Myasthenic Syndrome (CMS) caused by a defect in AChR function or clustering, specifically linked to the *RAPSN* gene.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Congenital Myasthenic Syndromes (CMS):** A heterogeneous group of inherited disorders affecting the NMJ, leading to muscle weakness and fatigability. Unlike Myasthenia Gravis (MG), CMS is typically present from birth or early childhood and is not autoimmune.
*   **Pathophysiology of *RAPSN*-related CMS:** Rapsyn is a cytoskeletal protein that links AChRs to the subsynaptic lattice. Mutations in *RAPSN* (most commonly heterozygous mutations) lead to reduced AChR density at the postsynaptic membrane. This results in fewer available receptors for ACh binding, causing impaired neuromuscular transmission. The excess AChRs are often found clustered abnormally in the synaptic cleft region, as seen on the biopsy.
*   **Electrophysiology:** RNS typically shows a decremental response due to the reduced number of functional AChRs. Post-exercise facilitation is often seen because brief exercise increases the release of ACh, temporarily overcoming the receptor deficit.
*   **Muscle Biopsy:** Histology is often normal, but special stains (e.g., anti-AChR antibodies) can show abnormal AChR distribution (clustering in the cleft).
*   **Molecular Defect:** The primary defect is the impaired clustering and stabilization of AChRs at the postsynaptic membrane due to the dysfunctional rapsyn protein.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deficiency in acetylcholinesterase (AChE)** would lead to *increased* ACh levels in the synaptic cleft, causing cholinergic crisis symptoms (excessive salivation, muscle cramps, fasciculations, paralysis) rather than fatigable weakness. AChE deficiency is extremely rare and presents differently. This option describes the opposite effect on ACh levels.
- (B): **Autoimmune destruction of postsynaptic AChRs** is the hallmark of Myasthenia Gravis (MG). MG typically presents later in life (though can occur in children), is autoimmune (presence of AChR antibodies), and often responds dramatically to immunosuppression. The patient's presentation since birth, family history, normal biopsy (no inflammation), and *RAPSN* mutation are inconsistent with MG.
- (D): **Impaired release of acetylcholine from the presynaptic nerve terminal** is characteristic of Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS) or certain CMS subtypes (e.g., those involving proteins like SNAP-25, syntaxin, or calcium channels). LEMS typically shows an *incremental* response on RNS (improves with repeated stimulation) and is often associated with small cell lung cancer. The patient's *decremental* response and *RAPSN* mutation rule this out.
- (E): **Mutation in the gene encoding the voltage-gated sodium channel (e.g., *SCN4A*)** typically causes periodic paralysis or myotonia, not the fatigable weakness characteristic of NMJ disorders. Nerve conduction studies would likely be abnormal in primary channelopathies affecting nerve excitability, which were normal in this patient.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Congenital Myasthenic Syndrome (CMS) | Myasthenia Gravis (MG) | Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS) | Acetylcholinesterase Deficiency | *RAPSN*-related CMS |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Onset**               | Congenital/Early Childhood           | Variable (often adult) | Variable (often adult)                   | Rare                          | Congenital         |
| **Etiology**            | Genetic                              | Autoimmune           | Autoimmune (presynaptic) / Paraneoplastic | Genetic (rare)                | Genetic (*RAPSN*)  |
| **Antibodies**          | Absent                               | Present (AChR, MuSK)  | Present (VGCC)                           | Absent                        | Absent             |
| **RNS**                 | Decremental (often + post-exercise)  | Decremental          | Incremental (often + post-exercise)       | N/A                           | Decremental       |
| **Biopsy**              | Normal/AChR clustering               | Normal               | Normal                                   | Normal                        | Normal/AChR cluster|
| **Molecular Defect**    | Postsynaptic (AChR, MuSK, etc.)     | Postsynaptic (AChR)   | Presynaptic (VGCC, release)              | Enzyme defect                 | AChR clustering   |
| **Key Gene**            | *RAPSN*, *CHRNE*, *COLQ*, etc.      | N/A                  | *CACNA1S*                                | N/A                           | *RAPSN*            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Congenital Myasthenic Syndromes (CMS) are a group of genetic disorders affecting the neuromuscular junction, presenting with fatigable weakness from birth or early childhood. Differentiating CMS from acquired conditions like Myasthenia Gravis (autoimmune, AChR antibodies) and Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (presynaptic, incremental RNS) is crucial. Specific genetic defects, like *RAPSN* mutations, lead to impaired AChR clustering, resulting in a reduced number of functional receptors at the postsynaptic membrane. Key diagnostic clues include early onset, family history, characteristic EMG/RNS findings (decremental response, post-exercise facilitation), and specific biopsy findings (AChR clustering).

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 02-49 (InflammatoryNeuropathy)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), specifically the molecular target of the autoantibodies in the most common variant, acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (AIDP), and its downstream effects on nerve conduction. (本题考察的是吉兰-巴雷综合征 (GBS) 的病理生理学，特别是最常见变体急性炎症性脱髓鞘性多神经病 (AIDP) 中自身抗体的分子靶标及其对神经传导的下游影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to the emergency department with rapidly progressive, ascending symmetric weakness starting in his lower extremities over the past 7 days. He reports paresthesias in his hands and feet. He denies fever, headache, or recent trauma. His past medical history is significant for hypertension treated with lisinopril and hyperlipidemia treated with atorvastatin. He recalls having a severe upper respiratory infection with diarrhea approximately 3 weeks prior to the onset of weakness. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals decreased deep tendon reflexes (areflexia) in the lower extremities and diminished reflexes in the upper extremities. Motor strength is 2/5 in the distal lower extremities and 4/5 in the proximal lower extremities. Upper extremity strength is 5/5. Sensory examination shows decreased light touch sensation in a stocking-glove distribution. Nerve conduction studies (NCS) show slowed conduction velocity and conduction block in multiple peripheral nerves, with normal sensory nerve action potentials (SNAPs). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis reveals albuminocytologic dissociation (elevated protein with normal white blood cell count).

Which of the following molecular targets is most likely involved in the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Voltage-gated sodium channels
(B) Acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction
(C) Myelin basic protein (MBP)
(D) Voltage-gated potassium channels
(E) Schwann cell surface gangliosides

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male with rapidly progressive, ascending symmetric weakness and paresthesias over 7 days. This pattern is classic for GBS.
- **Preceding Illness:** History of upper respiratory infection with diarrhea 3 weeks prior. This is a common trigger for GBS, often associated with *Campylobacter jejuni* infection.
- **Neurological Examination:** Areflexia (loss of deep tendon reflexes) and symmetric weakness, particularly in distal lower extremities, are hallmark signs of GBS. Sensory changes (stocking-glove paresthesias) are also common.
- **Nerve Conduction Studies (NCS):** Slowed conduction velocity and conduction block are characteristic findings in demyelinating neuropathies like AIDP, the most common form of GBS. Normal SNAPs suggest the primary pathology is demyelination affecting motor nerves, although sensory nerves can also be involved.
- **Cerebrospinal Fluid (CSF):** Albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal cells) is a classic finding in GBS, typically appearing after the first week of symptoms.
- **Overall Picture:** The clinical presentation, preceding infection, neurological findings (ascending weakness, areflexia), NCS results (demyelination), and CSF findings strongly point towards Guillain-Barré syndrome, specifically the acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (AIDP) variant.
- **Question Stem:** The question asks for the molecular target involved in the pathogenesis. In AIDP, the immune system attacks myelin components of peripheral nerves.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **GBS Pathogenesis:** GBS is an autoimmune disorder where the immune system mistakenly attacks the peripheral nervous system.
- **AIDP Mechanism:** In AIDP, the primary target is the myelin sheath surrounding peripheral nerve axons. Autoantibodies and T-cells target myelin components.
- **Molecular Targets:** The most common molecular targets in AIDP are gangliosides, specifically GM1, GD1a, and GalNAc-GD1a, which are located on the surface of Schwann cells and myelin. These gangliosides are structurally similar to lipooligosaccharides (LOS) found on *Campylobacter jejuni*, leading to molecular mimicry and triggering the autoimmune response after infection. Antibodies bind to these gangliosides, leading to complement activation, macrophage infiltration, and ultimately demyelination.
- **Other GBS Variants:** While AIDP is the most common, other variants exist (e.g., acute motor axonal neuropathy - AMAN, acute motor and sensory axonal neuropathy - AMSAN). AMAN and AMSAN are often associated with anti-GM1 antibodies but target axonal components (like voltage-gated sodium channels or gangliosides on the axon) rather than myelin. However, the classic AIDP presentation described here points towards myelin/Schwann cell targets.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Voltage-gated sodium channels:** These are the primary target in AMAN, a variant of GBS characterized by axonal damage rather than demyelination. Patients with AMAN typically have normal or only mildly slowed conduction velocities on NCS, unlike the significant slowing and conduction block seen in this patient (AIDP).
- (B) **Acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction:** These are the target in Myasthenia Gravis, an autoimmune disorder causing fluctuating muscle weakness, typically affecting ocular, bulbar, and limb muscles. It does not cause areflexia or the characteristic CSF findings of GBS. NCS in Myasthenia Gravis are typically normal, although repetitive nerve stimulation may show a decremental response.
- (C) **Myelin basic protein (MBP):** MBP is a major protein component of myelin in the central nervous system (CNS), not the peripheral nervous system (PNS). While antibodies against MBP can be found in some CNS demyelinating diseases like multiple sclerosis, they are not the primary target in GBS/AIDP, which affects PNS myelin. The main targets in AIDP are peripheral nerve components like Schwann cell surface gangliosides.
- (D) **Voltage-gated potassium channels:** These are primarily involved in repolarization of the action potential. While antibodies against certain potassium channels (e.g., Kv1.4) have been implicated in some rare forms of periodic paralysis or neuromyotonia, they are not the characteristic molecular target in the common forms of GBS, particularly AIDP.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Guillain-Barré Syndrome (AIDP) | Myasthenia Gravis | Acute Motor Axonal Neuropathy (AMAN) | Multiple Sclerosis (MS) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------|--------------------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Primary Target**   | PNS Myelin/Schwann Cells        | NMJ ACh Receptors | PNS Axons (Sodium Channels)         | CNS Myelin               |
| **Key Molecular Target** | Gangliosides (GM1, GD1a)       | AChR subunits    | GM1 Ganglioside (anti-GM1 Ab)        | Myelin Basic Protein (MBP) |
| **Clinical Features** | Ascending weakness, Areflexia   | Fluctuating weakness, Ptosis, Diplopia | Proximal weakness, Areflexia        | CNS deficits (optic neuritis, sensory loss, motor weakness, ataxia) |
| **NCS Findings**     | Demyelination (slowed velocity, conduction block) | Normal (or decremental response on RNS) | Axonal loss (reduced amplitude)     | CNS demyelination (prolonged latency) |
| **CSF**              | Albuminocytologic dissociation  | Normal            | Normal or mild protein elevation     | Oligoclonal bands, elevated IgG |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), particularly the AIDP variant, is an autoimmune attack on peripheral nerve myelin, often triggered by preceding infection. The key molecular targets are Schwann cell surface gangliosides (like GM1, GD1a). Differentiating GBS from other neuromuscular disorders like Myasthenia Gravis (NMJ target, AChR) or axonal variants of GBS (AMAN targeting sodium channels) requires understanding the specific molecular targets and resulting electrophysiological findings. Remember the classic presentation: rapidly progressive ascending symmetric weakness, areflexia, preceding infection, albuminocytologic dissociation in CSF, and demyelinating features on NCS.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 02-50 (Guillain Barré syndrome is an autoimmune disease l)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) is an acute autoimmune polyradiculoneuropathy often triggered by infection, characterized by ascending paralysis due to immune-mediated attack on peripheral nerve myelin or axons, leading to demyelination or axonal damage. The primary molecular target in the most common form (AIDP) is myelin components. (GBS 是一种急性自身免疫性多发性神经根炎，通常由感染触发，其特征是由于对周围神经髓鞘或轴突的免疫介导攻击引起的上升性瘫痪，导致髓鞘脱落或轴突损伤。最常见形式（AIDP）的主要分子靶标是髓鞘成分。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of rapidly progressive bilateral lower extremity weakness that started 3 days ago. He reports tingling sensations in his feet and hands that began a week prior. He recalls having a severe upper respiratory infection with diarrhea approximately 2 weeks before the onset of neurological symptoms. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals symmetric ascending weakness, areflexia in the lower extremities, and diminished reflexes in the upper extremities. Sensory examination shows decreased light touch sensation distally in a stocking-glove distribution. Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis shows albuminocytologic dissociation (elevated protein with normal white blood cell count). Nerve conduction studies (NCS) demonstrate slowed conduction velocities and conduction block in multiple peripheral nerves.

Which of the following is the most likely molecular target of the autoimmune attack in this patient?

(A) Voltage-gated sodium channels
(B) Acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction
(C) Myelin basic protein (MBP) and P2 protein
(D) Voltage-gated potassium channels
(E) Aquaporin-4 channels

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male. Age is typical for GBS.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Rapidly progressive bilateral lower extremity weakness (ascending paralysis) starting 3 days ago. This is a hallmark symptom of GBS.
- **Preceding Event:** Upper respiratory infection with diarrhea 2 weeks prior. This suggests a preceding infection (often *Campylobacter jejuni* for diarrhea, or viral URI) which is a common trigger for GBS.
- **Neurological Examination:** Symmetric ascending weakness, areflexia (lower extremities), diminished reflexes (upper extremities). This pattern is classic for GBS.
- **Sensory Examination:** Decreased light touch sensation distally (stocking-glove). Sensory involvement can occur in GBS, though motor symptoms are usually predominant.
- **CSF Analysis:** Albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal WBC count). This is a characteristic finding in GBS, typically appearing after the first week of symptoms.
- **Nerve Conduction Studies (NCS):** Slowed conduction velocities and conduction block. These findings indicate demyelination of peripheral nerves, consistent with the most common form of GBS (Acute Inflammatory Demyelinating Polyneuropathy - AIDP).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely molecular target of the autoimmune attack. Given the clinical presentation and NCS findings pointing towards AIDP, the target is likely a component of the myelin sheath.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Guillain-Barré Syndrome (GBS):** An acute autoimmune polyradiculoneuropathy.
- **Pathogenesis:** Molecular mimicry is often implicated. Antibodies generated against infectious agents (e.g., *Campylobacter jejuni* lipooligosaccharides) cross-react with components of peripheral nerves.
- **AIDP (Acute Inflammatory Demyelinating Polyneuropathy):** The most common subtype (~85% of cases in North America/Europe). Characterized by immune-mediated attack on myelin sheath components.
- **Molecular Targets in AIDP:** The primary targets are proteins within the myelin sheath, specifically Myelin Basic Protein (MBP) and P2 protein (peripheral nerve myelin glycoprotein). Antibodies against these proteins lead to complement activation and macrophage-mediated demyelination.
- **Other GBS Subtypes:**
    - Acute Motor Axonal Neuropathy (AMAN): Antibodies target axonal components, often gangliosides (e.g., GM1, GD1a) on the axon membrane, particularly at the nodes of Ranvier. Often associated with *C. jejuni*.
    - Acute Motor and Sensory Axonal Neuropathy (AMSAN): Similar to AMAN but affects both motor and sensory axons.
    - Miller Fisher Syndrome (MFS): Variant characterized by ophthalmoplegia, ataxia, and areflexia. Antibodies often target GQ1b ganglioside.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's presentation (ascending paralysis, areflexia, preceding infection) and NCS findings (demyelination) strongly suggest AIDP. Therefore, the molecular targets are likely myelin components.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Voltage-gated sodium channels:** These are the primary targets in Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS), an autoimmune disorder affecting the presynaptic neuromuscular junction, often associated with small cell lung cancer. LEMS presents with proximal muscle weakness that improves with exercise, autonomic dysfunction, and reduced or absent reflexes, but typically *not* ascending paralysis or albuminocytologic dissociation.
- (B) **Acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction:** These are the targets in Myasthenia Gravis (MG), an autoimmune disorder causing fluctuating muscle weakness that worsens with activity and improves with rest. MG primarily affects ocular, bulbar, and limb muscles, but does not typically cause ascending paralysis, areflexia, or albuminocytologic dissociation.
- (D) **Voltage-gated potassium channels:** These are the targets in Neuromyotonia (Isaac's syndrome), an autoimmune disorder characterized by continuous muscle fiber firing, leading to muscle stiffness, cramps, and fasciculations. It does not cause the typical ascending paralysis and areflexia seen in GBS.
- (E) **Aquaporin-4 channels:** These are the targets in Neuromyelitis Optica Spectrum Disorder (NMOSD), an autoimmune inflammatory disease primarily affecting the optic nerves and spinal cord, causing optic neuritis and transverse myelitis. It does not affect peripheral nerves in the manner described in GBS.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Guillain-Barré Syndrome (AIDP) | Myasthenia Gravis (MG) | Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS) | Neuromyotonia (Isaac's) | Neuromyelitis Optica (NMOSD) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Primary Target**      | Myelin (MBP, P2)                | ACh Receptor (NMJ)    | Voltage-gated Ca2+ channels (NMJ)       | Voltage-gated K+ channels | Aquaporin-4 (CNS)            |
| **Clinical Presentation** | Ascending paralysis, areflexia  | Fluctuating weakness   | Proximal weakness, improves w/ exercise | Stiffness, cramps       | Optic neuritis, myelitis     |
| **Reflexes**            | Areflexia/Hyporeflexia          | Normal                 | Hyporeflexia/Areflexia                   | Normal/Hyperreflexia    | Normal                       |
| **CSF**                 | Albuminocytologic dissociation | Normal                 | Normal                                   | Normal                  | Normal/Mild pleocytosis      |
| **NCS**                 | Demyelination (slowed velocity) | Normal/Decrement       | Normal/Increment                          | Normal                  | Normal                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Guillain-Barré syndrome, particularly the AIDP subtype, is characterized by an autoimmune attack on peripheral nerve myelin. The most common molecular targets are myelin basic protein (MBP) and P2 protein. Differentiating GBS from other neuromuscular disorders like MG (AChR), LEMS (VGCC), Neuromyotonia (VGKC), and NMOSD (AQP4) requires understanding their distinct clinical presentations, pathophysiology, and molecular targets. Remember the classic presentation of ascending paralysis, areflexia, and albuminocytologic dissociation for GBS.
